A floating resistor network has unique voltage drops but no absolute voltage reference. Derive its nullspace, test charge balance, and distinguish adding a computational gauge from changing the physical boundary conditions.

Chapter 18 · Counting vibration modes without calculating their shapes

A long chain of beads is tested on a shaker. The instrument can sweep only a narrow frequency interval, and the operator wants to know which resonance lies inside it. Turn a mass and spring model into a counting problem. Work out why a sign in an elimination step can identify a frequency that has never been computed directly.

Start with one displacement per bead

Number the moving beads from one to \(n\). A fixed support at either end has zero displacement. Each neighbouring pair is joined by an identical linear spring of stiffness \(k\), including the two end springs. Write down the force on bead \(i\) by extending each spring separately. The left spring pulls in proportion to \(u_{i-1}-u_i\), the right spring in proportion to \(u_{i+1}-u_i\). The diagonal coefficient two comes from two neighbours, including a fixed support when the bead is at an end. Removing an end spring would change the physical model and its first frequency.

\[m_i\ddot u_i+k(2u_i-u_{i-1}-u_{i+1})=0,\]
\[u_0=u_{n+1}=0.\]

Set \(u_i(t)=v_i\cos(\omega t)\) and cancel the nonzero time factor. The resulting equation is \(Kv=\lambda Mv\) with \(\lambda=\omega^2\). The stiffness matrix has a quadratic energy interpretation: multiplying by a displacement on either side gives the sum of all squared spring extensions times \(k\). This sum is positive unless all displacements vanish, because the end supports pin the chain. The diagonal mass matrix is also positive. Thus a frequency of zero in this experiment would call for an explanation.

Directly dividing each row by its own mass produces \(M^{-1}K\), which need not look symmetric. Instead set \(z=M^{1/2}v\). Multiplying by \(M^{-1/2}\) on the left gives \(Az=\lambda z\) with \(A=M^{-1/2}KM^{-1/2}\). The diagonal entries are \(a_i=2k/m_i\); the neighbouring entries are \(b_i=-k/\sqrt{m_im_{i+1}}\). Both have units of inverse time squared. Increasing one bead mass changes two neighbouring off-diagonals as well as a diagonal. Changing only the diagonal in a program would simulate a different chain.

Derive the shift count by completing squares

Pick a real test value \(s\) in squared-frequency units. In an orthonormal eigenbasis the quadratic form of \(A-sI\) is \(\sum_j(\lambda_j-s)y_j^2\). The largest dimension of a subspace on which this form is strictly negative counts eigenvalues below the test value. An invertible change of coordinates maps a negative subspace bijectively onto another negative subspace of the same dimension. The largest dimension stays fixed. This is the part of the law of inertia that our computation needs.

Now look at the same form in the original bead coordinates. Its first terms are \(d_1 z_1^2+2b_1z_1z_2+(a_2-s)z_2^2\), where \(d_1=a_1-s\). Complete the square in \(z_1\). The coefficient of the transformed first square is \(d_1\). The coefficient left for \(z_2^2\) is reduced by \(b_1^2/d_1\). Only the next coordinate is touched because the matrix is tridiagonal. Repeat the same operation until no cross term remains.

\[d_1=a_1-s,\]
\[d_{i+1}=a_{i+1}-s-\frac{b_i^2}{d_i}.\]

Each square completion is an invertible shear when its pivot is nonzero. Consequently the number of negative pivots equals the number of negative eigenvalues of the shifted matrix. This gives a practical count with linear work per shift and only one saved pivot. It also explains why the signs of the unmodified diagonal alone cannot work. Every spring couples two neighbouring trial directions; elimination carries the contribution of that coupling forward.

Find the bracket for a requested resonance

Put \(k=m_i=1\) in a three-bead chain. Its diagonal is \(2,2,2\) and both off-diagonals equal \(-1\). At \(s=1\) the second pivot is exactly zero, so choose \(s=1.2\) first to follow ordinary arithmetic. The pivots are \(0.8,-0.45,3.0222\ldots\). There is one eigenvalue below 1.2. At \(s=2.4\) they are \(-0.4,2.1,-0.87619\ldots\), giving two eigenvalues below 2.4. The second resonance in squared-frequency units lies between these two shifts. The uncertainty in angular frequency is the difference between their square roots.

For a longer chain it would be inconvenient to choose the first two trial shifts by luck. In an eigenvector choose a component of maximum absolute value. Divide its matrix row by that component. The sum of the neighbouring contributions is bounded in magnitude by the sum of the neighbouring absolute off-diagonals. Every eigenvalue therefore falls inside at least one interval centred on a diagonal entry with that row sum as radius. The minimum left endpoint and the maximum right endpoint supply an enclosing interval. A small outward extension avoids ties at the first or last bound.

To select the \(j\)-th eigenvalue in ascending order, halve the interval. If the count at its midpoint is less than \(j\), the desired eigenvalue is on the upper side. Otherwise keep the lower side. Once the interval is initialised, the reduction factor does not depend on the accuracy of an initial frequency guess. After \(r\) splits the width in exact arithmetic equals its original width divided by \(2^r\). A small eigenvalue can still have a relatively wide frequency interval after taking square roots, so apply the stopping criterion in the quantity your instrument actually reports.

Separate the numerical count from an experimental identification

For the uniform chain try \(v_i=\sin(i\theta)\). This trial shape vanishes at the left support. The right support requires

\[\theta=j\pi/(n+1).\]

Substitution into an interior row and the sum formula for neighbouring sines give

\[\lambda_j=4(k/m)\sin^2[j\pi/(2(n+1))].\]

For three unit beads the spectrum is approximately 0.58579, 2 and 3.41421. This furnishes an independent reference for all the mode counts, including the mode with an exactly representable eigenvalue. The notebook also compares with a dense symmetric solve which can be repeated using OneSimulation's linear algebra backend.

Double the mass of the middle bead and think about the second uniform mode before solving again. Its displacement vector is proportional to \((1,0,-1)\). There is a node at the heavier bead. Both \(Kv\) and \(Mv\) are unchanged for this particular vector, so its squared frequency remains two. The lowest mode has substantial displacement there and generally moves. The operator can use this contrast experimentally: a frequency that hardly shifts under a known local mass change constrains the possible mode shape even when that shape was never recorded.

The count itself describes the modelled spectrum. A measured frequency response can hide a resonance if the shaker or the sensor sits exactly on a modal node. Conversely, a broad peak may contain two lightly separated modes. To compare with an experiment one needs the forcing and measurement directions as well as the frequency count. In the symmetric ideal chain, replacing a centre sensor with two off-centre sensors can expose a mode that a centre sensor cannot see. The counting algorithm has no term for this observation geometry.

What a zero pivot means on a computer

A zero intermediate pivot need not be an eigenvalue of the entire matrix. It may be an eigenvalue of a smaller leading block. Approaching the troublesome shift from one side resolves the ambiguous endpoint convention. If instead one jumped directly to an arbitrary large replacement pivot, its coupling contribution and sometimes its sign count would be wrong. The notebook uses a small negative pivot in unit-scaled problems, as in a conventional safeguarded Sturm count. An exact eigenvalue at the shift is then counted on the lower side. It tests that convention on the known three-bead chain.

Floating-point arithmetic places another limit on what it means to halve a bracket. Eventually the rounded midpoint equals one of its endpoints. At that stage further iterations cannot separate floating-point trial values. A production interval solver also handles extreme scaling, split tridiagonal blocks and tiny off-diagonals, and can report an interval containing a cluster. The notebook is deliberately a transparent solver for moderate, nondimensional examples. Its final consistency test tolerates a few units of roundoff so that the dense reference can be rounded differently.

Open editable notebook Download .ipynb

Tutorial on counts, brackets and experimental nodes

1. A four-second sweep is centred at an angular frequency of 1.4 per second. Does the three-bead example put the second natural frequency between 1.40 and 1.43 per second?

Work through the solution

Square both positive bounds to get 1.96 and 2.0449 in inverse seconds squared. The count changes from one to two across them because the middle eigenvalue is 2. Its frequency is the square root of two, approximately 1.41421 per second. A finite measurement time still limits how sharply an instrument can identify this peak.

2. A starting interval in squared frequency is 8 units wide. How many halvings guarantee a width below one thousandth of a unit, and what part of the cost grows with bead count?

Work through the solution

Thirteen halvings give

\[8/8192=0.0009765625.\]

Twelve leave \(0.001953125\). Each test visits every diagonal and each connecting off-diagonal once. The number of halvings follows from the desired absolute accuracy; the work of each count grows with the length of the chain.

3. Break the spring between beads 7 and 8 of a fixed-end chain without adding a support. How should the inertia procedure and the stiffness entries change?

Work through the solution

Remove the coupling and subtract that spring’s stiffness from each of the two adjacent unscaled diagonal entries before mass normalisation. The two components now have an independently free end and a fixed outer support. Count the shifted pivots separately in the two blocks and add the counts. Simply setting the off-diagonal to zero while keeping both diagonals would add artificial restoring forces at the cut.

Problem sheet 7 · Project · Can two displacement sensors distinguish soft and stiff modes?

A pair of spring-mounted platforms forms a small inverse experiment. Use energy, conditioning, factorisation and modal residuals to decide what can be inferred from a measured displacement.

Assemble the stiffness from a physical experiment

Each platform is attached to a wall by a spring of stiffness \(k=1\) N/m. A coupling spring between platforms has stiffness \(c=49.5\) N/m. Start with the extensions of all three springs, write twice the energy and identify \(K\). Predict the two eigenvectors before computing its eigenvalues.

Work through the solution

The extension of the coupling is \(x_2-x_1\). Thus \(2U=k(x_1^2+x_2^2)+c(x_2-x_1)^2\) and the diagonal entries are \(k+c=50.5\), with off-diagonals \(-c=-49.5\). In-phase motion leaves the coupling untouched and has eigenvalue 1. Opposing motion stretches it and has eigenvalue 100. The 2-norm condition number is 100.

Solve one load and verify the result independently

Apply \(f=(1,0)\) N. Solve in modal coordinates, then calculate the elimination pivots and the diagonal of the Cholesky factor and recover the same displacements by a separate route. Which part of \(x\) is most difficult to infer from imperfect displacement measurements?

Work through the solution

The load is half the sum of \((1,1)\) and \((1,-1)\). Divide by their stiffnesses to get \(x=(0.505,0.495)\) m. The first pivot is 50.5, the second is

\[\det K/50.5=100/50.5=1.980198.\]

Eliminating the first row yields this second pivot with right-hand side \(49.5/50.5\). These elimination pivots are the diagonal of \(D\) in an \(LDL^T\) factorization. The positive-diagonal Cholesky factor has their square roots on its diagonal, about 7.106 and 1.407. Back substitution agrees. The difference of the displacements is only 0.01 m. Estimating the large coupling stiffness from that difference is sensitive to small sensor errors.

Change the energy and test the diagnosis

Divide \(K\) by 50.5 to give a matrix with unit diagonal. Enter the new off-diagonal into the chapter on positive pivots to the nearest slider value and compare the minimum across slices. Next use the scaling experiment with equal diagonal stiffnesses and the absolute coupling. Explain the residual slow direction.

Work through the solution

The normalised coupling is about \(-0.9802\). Its second pivot is \(1-0.9802^2\simeq0.0392\). The almost-flat valley runs along the in-phase motion. Flipping the sign of the second coordinate changes the sign of the coupling and preserves eigenvalues. Diagonal preconditioning cannot separate the scales because the diagonal terms are already equal; a basis that separates the two physical modes can.

Transfer the conclusion to a reported frequency

A unit-mass trial mode is tilted 3° away from the soft mode. Predict its Rayleigh quotient and the frequency bias. Would a two-decimal-place frequency match identify the trial shape?

Work through the solution

For eigenvalues 1 and 100 the quotient is \(1+99\sin^2(3^\circ)\simeq1.2712\) s⁻². The angular frequency is about 1.1275 rad/s, an error close to 12.8%. At an independently verified angular frequency of 1.00 rad/s to the nearest 0.01, this two-mode model requires \(1+99\sin^2\theta<1.005^2\), hence \(|\theta|<0.58^\circ\) up to the arbitrary eigenvector sign. The reading bounds the tilt but does not determine its value or sign. A smaller eigenvalue gap would weaken that bound.

Problem sheet 8 · Can an eigensolver certify the pitch of a continuous string?

A taut string gives a known continuum spectrum. Use its kinetic and elastic energies to construct two different matrix pencils. Both can pass a residual test to machine precision while assigning visibly different pitches to the same oscillation.

Build both matrices from a single element

A straight string of length \(L=1\) m, tension \(S=40\) N and mass per unit length \(\rho=0.0040\) kg/m has fixed ends. Divide it into \(N\) intervals of length \(h=L/N\). Approximate transverse displacement on each interval by a straight line between neighbouring nodal displacements \(u_i,u_{i+1}\). Use this shape for both velocity and slope. Which local mass entries would be lost by assigning half the segment mass to each end before squaring the interpolated velocity?

Integrate the kinetic and elastic quadratic forms

Derive the two \(2\times2\) matrices and explain which physical motion makes the elastic energy vanish.

Work through the solution

The interpolated velocity at local coordinate \(\xi\in[0,1]\) is \((1-\xi)\dot u_i+\xi\dot u_{i+1}\). The squared shape functions each integrate to \(1/3\) and their product integrates to \(1/6\). The slope is constant, \((u_{i+1}-u_i)/h\). Comparing the integrals with half a matrix quadratic form gives

\[M_e=\frac{\rho h}6\begin{pmatrix}2&1\\1&2\end{pmatrix},\qquad K_e=\frac S h\begin{pmatrix}1&-1\\-1&1\end{pmatrix}.\]

A common translation costs no elastic energy and a uniform velocity gives exactly the total element mass. Replacing \(M_e\) by its diagonal of row sums produces two equal masses \(\rho h/2\). This lumped version also gets the uniform-motion energy right but gives more kinetic energy for velocities of opposite sign at the nodes. Assemble contributions at shared nodes and eliminate fixed endpoint degrees of freedom after applying the local row sum. Summing rows only after deleting endpoint columns silently changes the end-adjacent mass.

Diagonalise a sparse pencil without forming an inverse

The interior stiffness row applies \(S(2u_j-u_{j-1}-u_{j+1})/h\). Set \(u_j=\sin(j\theta)\) so

\[u_{j-1}+u_{j+1}=2\cos\theta\,u_j.\]

What does each mass row do to the same nodal vector, and which wavenumbers obey both boundary conditions?

Find both discrete dispersion relations

Impose \(u_0=u_N=0\), discard the zero vector and express the angular frequencies directly.

Work through the solution

Use \(\theta=m\pi/N\) for \(m=1,\ldots,N-1\). The consistent mass row multiplies the nodal sine by \(\rho h(2+\cos\theta)/3\); the lumped row multiplies it by \(\rho h\). Inserting \(u(t)=v e^{i\omega t}\) into \(M\ddot u+Ku=0\) gives

\[Kv=\omega^2 Mv.\]

With \(c=\sqrt{S/\rho}=100\) m/s the results are

\[\omega_{\rm L}=\frac{2c}{h}\sin(\theta/2),\]
\[\omega_{\rm C}=\omega_{\rm L}\sqrt{\frac3{2+\cos\theta}},\]
\[\omega_{\rm exact}=c\,m\pi/L .\]

The consistent mass includes the exact kinetic energy of each piecewise-linear trial displacement. For this conforming space its Rayleigh–Ritz eigenvalues bound the exact squared frequencies from above. The lumped result is lower because \(\sin u<u\) for positive \(u\). Lumping changes the inner product, so the same variational upper-bound argument cannot simply be transferred to it. For a nonuniform mesh the sine vectors need not diagonalise either matrix. A symmetric generalised eigensolver or a Cholesky reduction \(M=BB^T,\ B^{-1}KB^{-T}w=\lambda w\) retains the appropriate symmetry. Recover \(v=B^{-T}w\) and normalise in the mass inner product.

Distinguish algebraic and discretisation error

Audit a computed fourth harmonic

Use eight intervals. Both sine vectors give tiny relative residual \(\|Kv-\lambda Mv\|/[(\|K\|+|\lambda|\|M\|)\|v\|]\) in their own equations. Report the predicted frequency with each mass. Explain how an experimenter could spot the error without an exact continuum reference.

Work through the solution

Here \(\theta=\pi/2\). The continuum fourth harmonic is 200.0 Hz. The lumped value is \(800\sqrt{2}/(2\pi)=180.06\) Hz, and the consistent value is larger by \(\sqrt{3/2}\), giving 220.53 Hz. Each algebraic residual tests whether the vector solves the matrix equation supplied to the solver. Neither residual tests the geometric approximation used to build that equation. Refine while keeping the same physical mode number and track the same number of interior zeros. Comparing the highest available harmonic after every refinement compares different physical modes.

For a smooth fixed mode the frequency ratios expand as \(1-\theta^2/24+O(\theta^4)\) for lumping and \(1+\theta^2/24+O(\theta^4)\) for consistent mass. Doubling the number of intervals quarters the leading bias. Their mean cancels the leading term for this uniform constant-coefficient string. Such cancellation is a consequence of these specific expansions; on a different mesh, solve or measure the convergence order again. Checking mass orthogonality between the reported modes gives a separate test that an eigensolver has returned distinct vectors, particularly when modes approach a cluster.

Measure the usable part of a spectrum

The experiment plots the two percentage errors against mode number over the actual discrete spectrum. Move the interval count while keeping track of mode 4. Look for a mode with about 1% frequency error and estimate how many points per wavelength the mesh must supply. The optional blended mass \(M_\alpha=(1-\alpha)M_L+\alpha M_C\) gives another quantitative test of the asymptotic cancellation. Blending mass matrices differs from averaging the two eigenfrequencies at finite \(h\).

Open this experiment and change its inputs online

Predict a dispersion-cancelling blend

Expand the blended mass multiplier and find the value of \(\alpha\) cancelling the leading smooth-mode frequency error. Why can it still give misleading high harmonics?

Work through the solution

The mass multiplier divided by \(\rho h\) is

\[1-\alpha(1-\cos\theta)/3=1-\alpha\theta^2/6+\cdots.\]

Its inverse square root contributes \(+\alpha\theta^2/12\) to the frequency ratio. Multiplying by the lumped sine ratio gives leading coefficient \((2\alpha-1)/24\), which vanishes at \(\alpha=1/2\). At eight intervals mode 4 has a blended frequency \(180.06/\sqrt{5/6}=197.25\) Hz. The improvement is substantial but finite. As the mode approaches the grid scale, the discarded higher powers of \(\theta\) cease to be small; exact solution of the blended pencil cannot restore a sub-element waveform.

Matrix computations and spectral accuracy

Trace how algebraic structure, representation and numerical precision affect the solution of linear problems. Verify each conclusion with an independent diagnostic.

  1. When stable eigenvalues allow large transient growth

    Two damped states exchange energy in only one direction. Its eigenvalues predict ultimate decay, while a short-time response can grow by a large factor. Compute the matrix exponential, its gain and a resolvent-based sensitivity check.

  2. How a random sketch finds a dominant subspace

    An instrument returns a matrix too large to factor densely but cheap enough to multiply by a few vectors. Build a low-rank approximation by probing its range, explain what a random draw can miss and design a residual check using fresh probes.

  3. Solve a singular network without inventing a ground current

    A floating resistor network has unique voltage drops but no absolute voltage reference. Derive its nullspace, test charge balance, and distinguish adding a computational gauge from changing the physical boundary conditions.

  4. Refitting a sensor as observations arrive or disappear

    A calibration line receives one new observation at a time. Update its least-squares factorisation using rotations, quantify the influence of a deleted observation and detect when removing a sample destroys identifiability.

  5. Which resistor can carry its current through another path?

    A short circuit diagram leads to a matrix that describes both voltage differences and sensitivity to losing an edge. Change one conductance and follow where the current goes.

Matrix computations and spectral accuracy · 1. When stable eigenvalues allow large transient growth

Two damped states exchange energy in only one direction. Its eigenvalues predict ultimate decay, while a short-time response can grow by a large factor. Compute the matrix exponential, its gain and a resolvent-based sensitivity check.

Solve the evolution one component at a time

Consider dimensionless time \(t\) and state \(x=(x_1,x_2)^T\) with \(\dot x=Ax\), \(A=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}-1&K\\0&-2\end{smallmatrix}\right)\), for real \(K\ge0\). The second component decays independently; its transient drives the first. A physical example is a linearised pair of relaxation processes with one readout feeding another. Choose state units so the Euclidean norm represents the amplification of interest. If the coordinates measure unrelated quantities such as volts and kelvins, the unweighted norm changes if one simply changes units. A physical energy or covariance supplies a more appropriate weighted norm.

Starting from \(x(0)=(u,v)\), integrate the second row to find

\[x_2=e^{-2t}v.\]

The first row obeys \((e^t x_1)'=K e^{-t}v\), hence

\[e^{tA}=\begin{pmatrix}e^{-t}&K(e^{-t}-e^{-2t})\\0&e^{-2t}\end{pmatrix}.\]

Both eigenvalues are negative and the whole matrix exponential tends to zero. Yet for \(v=1,u=0\) the driven component reaches \(K/4\) at \(t=\ln2\), since \(e^{-t}-e^{-2t}\) is maximal there. With \(K=12\), the state at this instant is \((3,0.25)\) and its norm is 3.010. This is a lower bound on the maximum possible gain over unit input vectors and on the maximum over all times. The peak of the driven component need not coincide exactly with the peak of the total norm.

Find the input that grows most at a chosen time

For \(M=e^{tA}\), the squared output norm is \(x_0^TM^TMx_0\). Its maximum over unit vectors is the largest eigenvalue of \(M^TM\), hence the gain is the largest singular value of \(M\). For the triangular matrix above denote its entries by \(a,b,d\) on the first diagonal, off-diagonal and second diagonal. The characteristic polynomial of \(M^TM\) has trace \(s=a^2+b^2+d^2\) and determinant \(a^2d^2\). Its larger eigenvalue is \([s+\sqrt{s^2-4a^2d^2}]/2\). This direct formula lets a simulation compare a specified initial condition with the optimal combination at each time. The optimal right singular vector varies with time; a plot of the optimal curve is an envelope of different preparations.

Another test asks whether any instantaneous squared norm can increase at \(t=0\). Its derivative is \(x_0^T(A^T+A)x_0\). The largest eigenvalue of the symmetric part \(H=(A+A^T)/2\) is \((-3+\sqrt{1+K^2})/2\). It is positive precisely when \(K>\sqrt8\). If \(H\) is negative semidefinite every instantaneous state has a nonpositive norm derivative and the Euclidean semigroup is contractive. This criterion concerns energy growth in a specified norm and is distinct from asymptotic stability. At \(K=12\) the eigenvector of \(H\) giving fastest initial growth is also not generally the input giving largest long-time response.

Link a small singular value to sensitivity of eigenvalues

For \(z\) away from either eigenvalue, direct triangular inversion gives an off-diagonal resolvent entry \(K/[(z+1)(z+2)]\). Even at complex points visibly far from the eigenvalues the resolvent norm can be large. To see what that means, let \(\sigma_{\min}(zI-A)=\varepsilon\) with unit right and left singular vectors \(v,u\). Then \((zI-A)v=\varepsilon u\). The rank-one perturbation \(E=\varepsilon u v^*\) makes \((A+E)v=zv\), while its spectral norm is precisely \(\varepsilon\). Conversely any perturbation moving an eigenvalue to \(z\) must have norm at least \(\sigma_{\min}\). Therefore a contour of \(1/\|(zI-A)^{-1}\|\) maps the perturbation required for an eigenvalue there. This is the pseudospectrum construction. Physical perturbations may have additional sparsity or sign constraints, so unstructured contours describe the allowed full matrix perturbations of the stated norm.

Compare one preparation with the worst-case envelope

The experiment evaluates the exact triangular evolution and the largest singular value at each time. Change the coupling first, then rotate the unit initial input. At zero coupling both state components decay. At \(K=12\) with initial excitation only in the second coordinate the output norm exceeds three before decaying. Explain why the envelope can exceed every single displayed preparation at different times and why changing the input does not alter either eigenvalue.

Open the live experiment

How a detector can miss the strongest transient

The apparatus often measures a scalar trace \(y=c^Tx\). With preparation \(x(0)=(0,1)^T\) and a detector aligned with the first coordinate, \(y=K(e^{-t}-e^{-2t})\). A detector aligned with the second coordinate instead records \(e^{-2t}\), whose graph decreases immediately. Rotating the detector leaves the evolution untouched. At \(t=\ln 2\) and \(K=12\), two orthogonal recordings therefore give \(3\) and \(0.25\). Their sum of squares recovers \(9.0625\), so their joint norm reproduces the transient concealed in the second recording alone.

Given an input direction \(b\) and a detector \(c\), the measurable input-to-output amplitude is \(c^Te^{tA}b\). The optimal detector for that input is parallel to \(e^{tA}b\) by Cauchy–Schwarz. Maximising also over the preparation recovers the singular-value experiment above. Thus a discrepancy between a large theoretical bound and a small experimental trace can arise from either the excitation or the readout. An experiment that rotates only one of them cannot by itself test the unrestricted bound.

Tutorial · verify three distinct amplification diagnostics

Find the exact growth at one time

With \(K=8\), compare the driven component for input \((0,1)\) and the maximum Euclidean gain at \(t=\ln2\).

Work through the solution

The matrix entries are

\[a=0.5,b=2,d=0.25.\]

The specified input gives norm \(\sqrt{4.0625}=2.016\); the driven component itself is 2. The optimum follows from \(s=4.3125\) and \(a^2d^2=0.015625\), giving gain about 2.076. Selecting the unit right singular vector of the larger eigenvalue attains it. A direct check is to rotate a unit input around the circle and find the maximum by sampling increasingly fine angles.

Construct a structured perturbation placing an eigenvalue at zero

Keep both diagonal entries fixed and alter only \(A_{21}\) by \(\delta\). Determine the value needed for a zero eigenvalue and compare it with changing only a diagonal entry.

Work through the solution

The determinant becomes \(2-K\delta\). Thus \(\delta=2/K\), or \(1/6\) for \(K=12\). Changing only the first diagonal entry would require a shift of one, and changing only the second a shift of two. The smaller lower-left change closes a positive feedback loop. It need not be the smallest unrestricted spectral-norm perturbation, which is obtained from the singular value of \(-A\), but it explicitly demonstrates which physical feedback is implicated.

Decide which conclusion survives nondimensionalisation

Replace the coordinates by \(y=(x_1/10,x_2)\). How do the eigenvalues, coupling and interpretation of a Euclidean gain change?

Work through the solution

The similar matrix \(DAD^{-1}\), with \(D=\operatorname{diag}(0.1,1)\), has diagonal entries \(-1,-2\) and off-diagonal \(K/10\). The dynamics represent the same trajectories in different units; the eigenvalues agree. The bare Euclidean norm in \(y\) now downweights the original first component, so it can show much less gain. To recover the original norm use \(y^T D^{-T}D^{-1}y\). A statement about growth must name the norm and scales, especially when assessing whether a stable numerical model can amplify perturbations beyond a physical tolerance.

  • Oxford · Trefethen, Pseudospectra of linear operators

Matrix computations and spectral accuracy · 2. How a random sketch finds a dominant subspace

An instrument returns a matrix too large to factor densely but cheap enough to multiply by a few vectors. Build a low-rank approximation by probing its range, explain what a random draw can miss and design a residual check using fresh probes.

Start with the approximation that would be best if its subspace were known

Let \(A\) be an \(m\times n\) real data matrix. It can represent a collection of spatial snapshots, one per column. A reduced rank-\(k\) model stores \(k\) spatial patterns and a small coefficient matrix. If a matrix \(Q\) has orthonormal columns spanning a proposed range, then the closest approximation with columns in that range in Frobenius norm is \(QQ^TA\): each snapshot is independently orthogonally projected. Store \(B=Q^TA\); its singular-value decomposition is small when \(Q\) has relatively few columns. The distinction between finding the subspace and factoring within it is important. A perfect small SVD cannot recover a spatial direction missing from \(Q\).

If \(A=U\Sigma V^T\) is exactly rank \(k\), choose \(n\times k\) test matrix \(\Omega\) and form \(Y=A\Omega=U_k\Sigma_k V_k^T\Omega\). If the last \(k\times k\) factor is invertible, \(Y\) spans exactly the range of \(A\). Independent Gaussian columns make singularity a zero-probability event in exact arithmetic, because the rotated coefficients are again a continuous Gaussian matrix. This statement does not imply every draw has a well-conditioned factor, nor that a single probe spans every near-rank-\(k\) matrix. Orthogonalising \(Y\) with a numerically reliable QR yields the candidate basis \(Q\).

Derive the effect of gap and starting alignment

Consider rank reduction of \(A=\operatorname{diag}(10,1)\) with one unit probe \(\omega=(\cos\theta,\sin\theta)\). Its image is \((10\cos\theta,\sin\theta)\), so the angle \(\phi\) between the sampled direction and dominant left singular vector obeys \(\tan\phi=\tan\theta/10\) whenever \(\cos\theta\ne0\). A typical mixed direction is pulled toward the dominant range. A nearly perpendicular probe can still give a poor direction even with a substantial singular gap. For \(\theta=45^\circ\), \(\tan\phi=0.1\). The residual \((I-qq^T)A\) has only one nonzero singular value because its left range is one-dimensional. With \(q=(\cos\phi,\sin\phi)\), its norm squared is \(100\sin^2\phi+\cos^2\phi\), giving \(\sqrt{200/101}=1.407\). The optimal rank-one error is the discarded singular value 1.

One power step forms \(Y=(AA^T)A\Omega\), raising singular values to the third power in the sampled direction while the approximation is still formed for the original \(A\). The angle ratio shrinks to \(\tan\theta/1000\); the residual norm is then about 1.00005 for the same \(45^\circ\) probe. Additional steps require more passes over data. In finite precision reorthogonalise between multiplications so weak required directions are not erased by a large gap. If the target includes both leading and moderate directions, blindly powering a block without intermediate QR can make the very reduced subspace one wants numerically rank deficient.

Independently test an approximation after building it

Add \(p\) extra columns to \(\Omega\) to reduce the chance of poorly exciting a dominant combination. An oversampled \(Q\) has \(k+p\) columns; project to \(B=Q^TA\), take its dominant \(k\) small left singular vectors and map them back through \(Q\). For an exactly rank-\(k\) input the extra columns need appropriate numerical rank handling. In the nearly low-rank case they provide room to represent part of the tail and improve the final cut. Cost depends on access: computing \(A^TQ\) or performing every additional power pass can dominate on streamed or distributed data, even though the final dense SVD is tiny.

Freeze the fitted approximation \(\tilde A\) and consider an independent random sign vector \(g\) with entries equally likely \(\pm1\). Write \(R=A-\tilde A\). Since \(\mathbb E[gg^T]=I\),

\[\mathbb E\|Rg\|^2=\operatorname{tr}(R^TR)=\|R\|_F^2.\]

Multiple independent checks estimate residual energy and its sampling spread without explicitly assembling \(R\). This estimates Frobenius norm squared; the maximum directional error is a different objective. A single small response does not certify a small spectral norm or guarantee missed directions are unimportant to a particular downstream measurement. Test the physically relevant load or measurement directions as well.

Background · Why must the test vector be independent of the sketch?

Build the sampled range \(Q\) by orthogonalising \(A\Omega\). With exact arithmetic every sampled column already lies in the range of \(Q\). Applying the residual to those same vectors gives

\[(I-QQ^T)A\Omega=0.\]

For example, one column sampled from the two-dimensional identity spans only a line. It reconstructs its own training vector exactly while the unit vector perpendicular to it has error one. A fresh isotropic probe has, on average, nonzero energy in every missing direction. For a standard Gaussian vector \(g\) independent of \(Q\), the mean squared diagnostic is \(\mathbb E\|Rg\|^2=\|R\|_F^2\), with \(R=(I-QQ^T)A\). This identity follows by expanding \(g^TR^TRg\) and using \(\mathbb E[gg^T]=I\). One draw is still a noisy diagnostic, especially when only a single direction is missing.

Which starting direction defeats a random range probe?

For a diagonal matrix with singular values 1 and r, rotate a test vector through the dominant right singular direction. Each power iteration multiplies the tangent of its output angle by r². Set r to 0.45, compare zero and two iterations, then inspect the exactly orthogonal probe at 90°. Explain why its residual stays at one.

Open the live experiment

Tutorial · check an apparently good random approximation

Identify a failure when the test vector is reused

A matrix \(A\) is approximated by the orthogonal projection onto the range of \(A\Omega\). You test residuals only on the original columns of \(\Omega\) and find zeros. What does this certify?

Work through the solution

By construction \(QQ^TA\Omega=A\Omega\), up to roundoff if the full sampled range was kept. Thus the test is nearly tautological and does not measure error on independent directions. If \(\Omega\) has a missing dominant right singular direction, all these residuals can vanish while the leading reconstruction is poor. Use fresh random probes and targeted physical snapshots held out during fitting. If a final rank truncation is applied within \(Q\), the original probe residual need no longer vanish exactly but remains dependent on the fitted subspace.

Calculate a deliberately poor draw

Use \(A=\operatorname{diag}(10,1)\) and \(\tan\theta=100\). Compare the one-pass angle and residual with one power iteration.

Work through the solution

Initially \(\tan\phi=10\), so \(\sin^2\phi=100/101\) and the residual norm squared is \((10000+1)/101\), giving about 9.95. With one power step \(\tan\phi=0.10\), giving 1.407 instead. The bad starting direction had a small but nonzero overlap, so powering can recover it. An exactly perpendicular deterministic probe remains perpendicular after every power step. Oversampling adds independent opportunities to see a missed direction.

Design a stopping criterion for snapshot energies

Independent residual probes give squared norms \(1.0,1.4,0.8,1.6,1.2\) in scaled energy units, while independently known \(\|A\|_F^2=100\). Estimate the residual energy fraction and its sampling uncertainty. Does it imply a 1.2% relative error on every snapshot?

Work through the solution

The sample mean is 1.2 and sample standard deviation \(\sqrt{0.10}=0.316\), so the standard error is about 0.141 for independent probes. The estimated fraction of total squared Frobenius norm is \(1.20\%\), with approximate standard error 0.14 percentage points. The relative Frobenius norm itself is about \(\sqrt{0.012}=0.110\). Individual snapshots may fare much better or worse; the tests estimate a global aggregate. Five samples give only a rough uncertainty estimate, particularly if residual energy is concentrated in a few directions.

Further reading

  • Caltech · Halko, Martinsson and Tropp on randomised matrix decompositions
  • Course investigation · singular values and low-rank error

Matrix computations and spectral accuracy · 3. Solve a singular network without inventing a ground current

A floating resistor network has unique voltage drops but no absolute voltage reference. Derive its nullspace, test charge balance, and distinguish adding a computational gauge from changing the physical boundary conditions.

Build the conductance matrix as an energy Hessian

Orient each of the \(m\) connections of a connected graph and form an incidence matrix \(B\) with one row per edge, containing \(+1\) at its upstream node and \(-1\) at its downstream node. For node voltages \(v\), differences \(Bv\) determine currents \(i_e=g_e(Bv)_e\) in edges of conductance \(g_e>0\). Conservation at nodes reads \(B^Ti=b\), where externally injected currents \(b\) are positive into the network. Eliminating edge currents yields \(Lv=b,\ L=B^TGB\) with \(G=\operatorname{diag}(g_e)\). Reversing an edge changes a row of \(B\) and its current sign together; \(L\) and physical voltage predictions are invariant.

The identity \(v^TLv=\sum_e g_e(v_i-v_j)^2\ge0\) gives the nullspace directly. Equality forces equal voltage along each edge and hence constant voltage on a connected graph. Thus \(\ker L=\operatorname{span}\{\mathbf1\}\) and its range is \(\mathbf1^\perp\). A solution exists exactly when total injection is zero. On a graph with disconnected components the current sum must vanish separately on each component; one global cancellation can hide two incompatible islands. This is a compatibility condition on the physical data, distinct from the gauge freedom that persists for already compatible data.

Compare grounding, projection and a bordered solve

If \(b\) is compatible, choosing one node potential as zero removes one variable and one redundant conservation equation. The remaining principal submatrix is positive definite because a zero-energy vector extending with zero at the reference must vanish everywhere. An alternative chooses mean-zero voltage without favouring one node. Introduce a Lagrange multiplier \(\lambda\) and solve

\[\begin{pmatrix}L&\mathbf1\\\mathbf1^T&0\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}v\\\lambda\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}b\\0\end{pmatrix}.\]

The last equation fixes the gauge. Multiplying the first block by \(\mathbf1^T\) gives

\[\lambda=(\sum b_i)/n.\]

For consistent data \(\lambda=0\), so the bordered solution satisfies the original equations exactly and is the Moore–Penrose minimum-norm solution. For inconsistent data the solver effectively removes a uniform current \(\lambda\) from every node. This may be sensible as a least-squares projection under an explicitly chosen equal-error model, but it is not the same physical network as a returning current concentrated at an actual external lead. Retain and interpret the multiplier.

Another formulation works directly in the zero-mean subspace with projector \(P=I-\mathbf1\mathbf1^T/n\). Iterative methods can project the right side and reproject iterates to remove accumulated constant drift. A preconditioner must preserve the intended subspace or be followed by the appropriate projection. The gauge-invariant observable \(v_i-v_j\) is always preferable for comparing solvers with different references. Comparing raw voltages node by node can report a large error between mathematically equivalent physical solutions. A separate edge-current balance and dissipated power check validate more than one reference-dependent vector residual.

Verify a triangle by current conservation and work

Connect nodes 1–2 and 2–3 with 1 S each, and 1–3 with 0.5 S. Inject 1 A at node 1 and remove it at node 3. The direct branch has 2 Ω and the two-edge indirect branch also totals 2 Ω, so 0.5 A flows along each path. The terminal difference is 1 V. Taking \(v_3=0\) gives \(v=(1,0.5,0)\) V, while subtracting their mean yields \((0.5,0,-0.5)\) V. Both produce Joule power \(2(0.5^2/1)+(0.5^2/0.5)=1\) W. The terminal injection work is \(b^Tv=1\) W in either gauge because the sum of \(b\) vanishes.

A guard against concealed modelling error is to solve first with all conservation rows, reporting the incompatible component of \(b\); only then introduce the intended reference. If measured injected currents are \(1.00,0,-0.97\) A and node 3 is grounded by deleting its equation, the computed branch currents imply that node 3 actually returns 1.00 A. The measured 0.03 A mismatch is thereby absorbed entirely by the chosen reference. The bordered projection instead distributes 0.01 A equally among the nodes. A calibrated return-lead model or a covariance-weighted correction can choose between such interpretations. A small residual after row deletion alone cannot tell whether the three measured currents were physically consistent.

What changes when a floating voltmeter is re-zeroed?

Connect three nodes by a triangle. The 1–3 and 2–3 conductances are each 1 S. Inject the selected current at node 1 and remove it at node 3. The graph sweeps a common reference-voltage shift. Use the oriented drop across branch 1–2 to predict its current, then compare the total injected power with the displayed dissipation.

The downloadable OneSimulation circuit solves the three resistors and current source twice, choosing a different reference node. The two absolute voltage reports change. Their branch drops must agree.

Open the live experiment

Tutorial · audit gauges, disconnected graphs and sensitivities

Find an effective resistance without choosing a reference

For connected \(L\), point injection pair \(b=I(e_a-e_b)\) and minimum-norm inverse \(L^+\), derive the effective resistance and verify its sign.

Work through the solution

The gauge-fixed solution is \(v=L^+b\), so terminal voltage divided by \(I\) is \(R=(e_a-e_b)^TL^+(e_a-e_b)\). Positive definiteness on the zero-sum subspace gives nonnegative \(R\), strictly positive for distinct connected nodes. In the example it is 1 Ω, independently confirmed by the parallel branches. Summing injected power \(I^2R\) matches edge dissipation.

Determine how much one connection is worth

What is the first derivative of the triangle's effective resistance with respect to the direct conductance? Compare a prediction for raising it by 0.01 S with a direct circuit reduction.

Work through the solution

For unit injection, differentiating \(Lv=b\) on the fixed gauge subspace gives

\[dR=-v^T(dL)v.\]

A single conductance change has \(dL=(e_i-e_j)(e_i-e_j)^Tdg\), so

\[dR/dg=-(v_i-v_j)^2.\]

The direct drop is 1 V per ampere, giving \(-1\) in compatible units. Prediction is \(R\simeq0.990\) Ω; exact parallel conductance becomes 1.01 S, so \(R=0.99010\) Ω. A positive conductance increment cannot increase effective resistance here.

Diagnose an island and an unobservable offset

Remove every link between two internally connected components carrying net injections \(+0.10,-0.10\) A. Is one ground constraint enough? What if both component sums are corrected to zero?

Work through the solution

The disconnected graph has two independent constant null vectors, one per component. Although the total current is zero, both component compatibility tests fail. No voltage gauge can make the missing inter-island return path appear. If both sums become zero, two independent gauge constraints still remain necessary for unique node voltages. Potential differences within either component are uniquely determined; differences across the two floating components remain arbitrary unless a physical reference or coupling is introduced.

Further reading

  • ETH Zürich · numerical methods for CSE, sparse systems and positive definite forms
  • Course investigation · constrained systems and Schur complements

Matrix computations and spectral accuracy · 4. Refitting a sensor as observations arrive or disappear

A calibration line receives one new observation at a time. Update its least-squares factorisation using rotations, quantify the influence of a deleted observation and detect when removing a sample destroys identifiability.

Rotate away a new observation while preserving the objective

Suppose \(A\) has full column rank \(p\), observations \(b\), and an orthogonal factorisation has already transformed the upper part of the problem into \(Rx=z\) with upper-triangular \(R\). Lower transformed rows carry residuals but no coefficients. Because the transformations are orthogonal,

\[\|Ax-b\|^2=\|Rx-z\|^2+s^2,\]

where \(s^2\) is the accumulated minimum residual sum of squares. Appending row \(a^T\) and response \(y\) requires minimising \(\|Rx-z\|^2+(a^Tx-y)^2+s^2\). One can update the small augmented triangle instead of reloading every original sample. This is particularly useful when storing raw observations is expensive and fits need refreshing repeatedly.

Write the new row directly below \([R\;z]\). At column \(j\), combine diagonal value \(r\) with bottom value \(q\) using a Givens rotation with \(c=r/\operatorname{hypot}(r,q),\ s=q/\operatorname{hypot}(r,q)\). Multiplication of the two selected rows by \(\bigl(\begin{smallmatrix}c&s\\-s&c\end{smallmatrix}\bigr)\) gives a nonnegative diagonal \(\sqrt{r^2+q^2}\) and zero in the bottom column. Apply the same rotation to every remaining column and to the right side. If both inputs vanish choose the identity and investigate numerical rank separately. A robust hypotenuse evaluation avoids overflow when \(r^2+q^2\) itself exceeds the available range even though its square root is representable.

After sweeping all \(p\) columns the bottom row is zero in coefficients and has transformed response \(\gamma\). The new triangular solve gives the updated fit and the minimum residual sum rises by \(\gamma^2\). Each step preserves a squared norm, so this identity is a direct independent audit of online accumulation. Weighted least squares fits into the same calculation by scaling the row and response by the square root of its weight first. Dynamic reweighting of historical observations is a different operation and generally requires preserved information about individual rows.

Use one-dimensional corrections for interpretation

The rank-one update also has a useful algebraic form without explicitly computing a dense inverse. Set \(G=A^TA\), \(w=G^{-1}a\) evaluated by two triangular solves with \(R\), and \(h=a^Tw\). After adding a point the normal matrix becomes \(G+aa^T\). Subtracting the old normal equations from the new ones gives \((G+aa^T)\delta x=a(y-a^Tx)\), so \(\delta x=w(y-a^Tx)/(1+h)\), since \((G+aa^T)w=(1+h)a\). The increment to residual sum of squares is \((y-a^Tx)^2/(1+h)\). This formula for diagnostics is consistent with the orthogonal update and shows explicitly how a surprising response interacts with geometric leverage.

As an example fit a line \(y=c+dt\) at times \(t=-1,0,+1\) with readings \(0,1,2\). The exact line is \(c=d=1\), with \(G=\operatorname{diag}(3,2)\). A new observation at \(t=2\) reports \(y=4\) rather than predicted 3. Here \(a=(1,2)^T\), \(w=(1/3,1)^T\), \(h=7/3\). The corrected coefficients are \(c=1.1,\ d=1.3\), and residual sum rises by \(1/(1+7/3)=0.30\). Substitution back into all four data gives residuals \(0.2,-0.1,-0.4,0.3\); their squared sum is indeed 0.30. The update has moved a previously exact historical fit because the new reading changed the estimated slope and intercept.

Distinguish a safe deletion from rank loss

For deleting a row already included in the fitted \(G\), use that row's in-sample leverage \(\ell=a^TG^{-1}a\). It lies between zero and one as a diagonal element of the orthogonal projection \(A(A^TA)^{-1}A^T\). If \(\ell<1\), the remaining-data fit changes by \(-G^{-1}a\,r/(1-\ell)\), where \(r=y-a^Tx\) is the residual while the point was present. The leave-one-out prediction residual is \(r/(1-\ell)\). A point with small in-sample residual can therefore be highly influential when nearly essential to identification. If \(\ell=1\), deletion loses rank, and a formula dividing by \(1-\ell\) is warning of a structural change. Downdating a triangular factor should use a well-conditioned dedicated downdate or refactor with rank detection when close to this boundary. Squaring a poorly conditioned design into \(G\) just for convenience can lose accuracy.

Refit the line after one influential observation

Begin with three calibration pairs on y = x at x = −1, 0 and 1. Place a fourth sensor farther from the centre and choose its deviation from the old line. Predict its training residual and the error made when the entire fourth observation is omitted. Move it farther out with a fixed nonzero offset. Which residual approaches zero and which retains the full offset? Set the offset to zero and check whether a high-leverage point must also have a large residual.

The OneSimulation model fits the full augmented observation matrix with its dense least-squares solver. Compare this independent refit with the one-row update on screen.

Open the live experiment

Tutorial · inspect an update from three angles

Perform a complete scalar QR update

Three readings of a constant have average 2 and residual sum 0.50. Add value 5 at equal weight. Find the updated average, residual increment and scalar triangle.

Work through the solution

Before appending,

\[R=\sqrt3,\ z=2\sqrt3.\]

With appended row 1 the new diagonal is 2. The rotated right side is \((\sqrt3/2)(2\sqrt3)+(1/2)5=5.5\), giving average 2.75. The bottom response is \((-1/2)(2\sqrt3)+(\sqrt3/2)5=1.5\sqrt3\), contributing 6.75 to sum of squares and giving new total 7.25. The mean from the raw sum is also \((6+5)/4=2.75\).

Reverse the four-point line update

For the newly appended point in the line example, infer its in-sample leverage from the original append leverage, then recover the original leave-one-out residual.

Work through the solution

Adding its own row changes leverage from \(h\) to \(\ell=h/(1+h)=0.70\), obtained by solving \((G+aa^T)^{-1}a=w/(1+h)\). Its current fitted value is \(1.1+2(1.3)=3.7\), giving residual 0.3. Dividing by \(1-\ell=0.3\) recovers the predicted deletion residual 1.0. Confusing \(h\) with \(\ell\) would incorrectly put a leverage larger than one inside a leave-one-out formula.

Detect a geometry where a zero residual is inevitable

Fit a line with many observations at \(t=0\) and exactly one at \(t=1\). Show why the latter's in-sample residual can be zero for any value and why its fit quality cannot validate the slope.

Work through the solution

All \(t=0\) samples determine the intercept by their mean. The unique nonzero time then fixes the slope to hit its own response exactly, so its residual is always zero and its in-sample leverage is one. Removing it leaves only one time coordinate and the slope unidentifiable. An extra independent observation at a distinct time, or a justified physical slope prior, is needed to test predictive slope accuracy. Increased repetitions at \(t=0\) reduce intercept noise without repairing this missing diagnostic.

Further reading

  • ETH Zürich · numerical methods for CSE, least squares and orthogonal factorisation
  • Course investigation · orthogonal reduction for least squares
  • Separate algebraic solver error from mesh error

Matrix computations and spectral accuracy · 5. Which resistor can carry its current through another path?

A short circuit diagram leads to a matrix that describes both voltage differences and sensitivity to losing an edge. Change one conductance and follow where the current goes.

Ground a node and derive the electrical leverage

Nodes A, B and C form a triangular loop. Node D connects only to C. Every edge is an ideal linear resistor. Orient an edge from node \(i\) to node \(j\) and give it an incidence vector \(b_e\) with a +1 at \(i\) and a −1 at \(j\). Its current under nodal voltages \(v\) is \(g_e b_e^T v\), where conductance \(g_e\) is measured in siemens. Kirchhoff's current law sums all outgoing currents into the net injection \(f\). It gives

\[Lv=f,\]
\[L=\sum_e g_e b_eb_e^T.\]

Adding a constant to every potential preserves all currents. Fix the potential of D at zero and remove its row and column. For a connected network the resulting grounded matrix is positive definite. Inject one ampere at one end of an edge and withdraw it at the other. With grounded coordinates the voltage difference per unit current is the effective resistance \(R_e=b_e^T L_g^{-1} b_e\). The fraction of this test current that flows through the tested edge is \(\ell_e=g_eR_e\). A bridge carries the full ampere. A parallel path reduces its share.

There is also a global check on the calculation. The sum of all the leverages is \(\mathrm{tr}(L_g^{-1}\sum_e g_e b_e b_e^T)=n-1\) on a connected graph with \(n\) nodes. Each leverage depends only on the graph and its conductances. The actual current in a specified operating condition also depends on the chosen source and sink.

Solve the triangle by hand

With 1 S on every branch, the detour A–C–B consists of two 1 Ω resistors in series. Inject 1 A at A and extract it at B. The detour's 0.5 S combines with the direct 1 S, so the measured drop is \(1/1.5=2/3\) V. The direct current is 2/3 A. The dead-end C–D branch carries zero current in this experiment. When tested between its own endpoints, however, C–D carries the entire test current. The four leverages are consequently \(2/3,2/3,2/3,1\) and sum to three.

Double A–B's conductance to 2 S. Its leverage rises to \(2/2.5=0.8\), while the voltage needed for the same 1 A drops to 0.4 V. This is why the two traces in the experiment have opposite slopes. Rerun with a weaker bridge. Its resistance increases, yet its leverage stays equal to one.

Exercise · remove the direct branch

How much does the terminal resistance rise when the 1 S direct edge is removed? What changes if the bridge is removed instead?

Work through the solution

For A and B the sole remaining connection consists of two 1 Ω resistors, so resistance rises from 2/3 Ω to 2 Ω, an increase of 4/3 Ω. Removing C–D instead isolates D. Measurements within the triangle remain finite, but a test injecting current at D and withdrawing it at C no longer has a finite DC voltage solution.

Exercise · differentiate the measured voltage

Under a fixed one-ampere A-to-B test, find the sensitivity of the terminal resistance to the direct conductance at 1 S.

Work through the solution

For variable \(g\) the resistance is \(1/(g+0.5)\). Differentiation gives \(-1/(g+0.5)^2=-4/9\) Ω/S at the chosen point. The magnitude equals the square of the voltage drop per ampere across that branch. Differentiating \(L_gv=f\) at fixed injection gives the same result for any branch, \(\partial (f^Tv)/\partial g_e=-(b_e^Tv)^2\) for the unit test.

Operators derived from a physical balance

Construct the matrix of a constrained network, a mechanical mode or a spatial field. Determine its nullspace, symmetry and natural inner product before choosing a factorisation.

How a continuum balance becomes a finite-element stiffness matrix

Engineering mechanics

A single bar element can predict an end displacement exactly and still miss the stress field inside. The difference follows directly from the balance it is asked to satisfy.

Turn a differential balance into work against a test displacement

Let a straight small-strain bar of length \(L\) have area \(A\), Young’s modulus \(E\), displacement \(u(x)\) and distributed axial load \(q(x)\) in N/m. Tensile axial resultant is \(N=EAu'\). Force balance on a slice gives \(N'+q=0\). Clamp the left end and prescribe a right-end force \(P\). Multiply the balance by any smooth virtual displacement \(v\) satisfying \(v(0)=0\), integrate along the bar, and integrate the derivative of \(N\) by parts.

\[\int_0^L EA\,u'v'\,dx=P\,v(L)+\int_0^L qv\,dx .\]

The boundary term at the clamp disappears because that displacement is prescribed. The unknown reaction can later be recovered by equilibrium. This formulation requires only first derivatives of trial displacements. Piecewise linear displacements are therefore admissible even though their slopes may jump at internal nodes.

Compute the stiffness rather than memorising it

On an element from \(x=0\) to \(h\), interpolate the two nodal displacements linearly:

\[u=(1-x/h)u_1+(x/h)u_2.\]

Let the test displacement have the same two basis functions. A virtual change of the first or second node gives the corresponding row of the balance. Both derivatives are constant with opposite signs.

\[B=\begin{bmatrix}-1/h&1/h\end{bmatrix},\qquad K_e=\int_0^h B^T EA B\,dx=\frac{EA}{h}\begin{bmatrix}1&-1\\-1&1\end{bmatrix}.\]

For a constant distributed load, each basis function integrates to \(h/2\), so each node receives equivalent load \(qh/2\). The two rows of stiffness sum to zero. A uniform translation stores no strain energy. At a shared node the neighbouring virtual-work contributions add because both elements refer to the same unknown displacement.

Worked example · compare a nodal value with the stress in a cell

Take a 1 m bar with \(EA=2.0\) MN, uniform \(q=1200\) N/m and free right end. Exact balance integrated from \(N(L)=0\) gives

\[N=q(L-x).\]

Integrating strain from the clamp produces \(u=q(Lx-x^2/2)/(EA)\). The free-end displacement is 0.300 mm. With a single element the free row of the discrete system has stiffness \(EA/L\) and load \(qL/2\), giving the same displacement. Yet its piecewise constant axial force is

\[EAu(L)/L=600\,\mathrm{N}.\]

The exact force ranges from 1200 N at the clamp to zero at the free end. The numerical force represents the mean over the cell.

Worked example · see what doubling resolution changes

Divide the bar into two equal elements. The middle node receives 600 N from both halves together, while the free node receives 300 N. The right element carries 300 N. Equilibrium of the middle node then puts 900 N in the left element. Their extensions are \(900(0.5)/(2\times10^6)=0.225\) mm and 0.075 mm. Nodal displacements agree with the exact quadratic at both nodes. The largest one-sided stress-resultant error within either element is now 300 N instead of 600 N. Comparing only tip displacements would hide this improvement.

Double the number of cells until the force error is below 100 N for the baseline load. Inspect the values on both sides of an element boundary and explain why agreement in tip displacement alone could have ended this refinement study too early.

Tutorial · find the support reaction from the discarded row

For the two-element calculation, recover the left reaction using the assembled loads and displacement. Then derive an expression for the maximum axial-force error with \(n\) equal elements. Why can two different mesh studies give two different conclusions?

Evaluate the clamp row and the local profile

The left internal nodal force is \(-900\) N. The left external distributed-load share is \(+300\) N. Equilibrium requires an additional reaction \(-1200\) N. On an element of width \(L/n\) the exact linearly varying force changes by \(qL/n\); its constant midpoint approximation misses an endpoint by \(qL/(2n)\). Tip displacement and maximum local force answer different engineering questions. Mesh convergence must therefore name its measured quantity.

Further reading · A. F. Bower, Applied Mechanics of Solids, §8.1, finite-element equations for elastic solids

Finding a displacement when no load acts in its direction

Engineering mechanics

A displacement gauge measures the lateral travel of a truss joint under a vertical load. A force balance determines the bar forces, but cannot directly give the sideways motion. Introduce a dummy load, differentiate the stored energy and recover the gauge reading with its sign.

Link an external force coordinate to stored strain energy

For a linear elastic pin-jointed truss, neglect support movement and temperature changes and assume small displacements. Bar i has constant axial force \(N_i\), length \(L_i\), modulus \(E_i\) and area \(A_i\). Integrating its force against its extension gives

\[U_i=N_i^2L_i/(2E_iA_i).\]

Superposition makes each force linear in the external loads. A small increment of load changes the equilibrium configuration. For a linear elastic conservative system the complementary energy equals U and its derivative with respect to an independent applied force gives displacement along that force.

\[U=\sum_i{N_i^2L_i\over2E_iA_i},\]
\[\delta_Q={\partial U\over\partial Q}=\sum_i {N_i n_i L_i\over E_iA_i},\]
\[n_i={\partial N_i\over\partial Q}.\]

The derivative \(n_i\) is the member force produced per unit trial load in the requested direction, with all other independent loads held fixed. Solve this trial equilibrium even if \(Q=0\) physically. Setting \(Q=0\) before differentiating erases its entire contribution. As a dimensional check, \(n_i\) is dimensionless when Q and \(N_i\) both use newtons, and the sum has dimensions of length.

Compute the two-bar flexibility matrix

A joint hangs from two overhead pins by straight bars slanting up-left and up-right, each at 45° to horizontal. Positive joint coordinates are rightward and downward. Let V be the applied downward force and H the rightward force; bars carry tension positive. Equilibrium gives

\[N_L=(V+H)/\sqrt2,\ N_R=(V-H)/\sqrt2.\]

Define bar axial compliances \(c_i=L_i/(E_iA_i)\). Insert the forces into the energy and differentiate with respect to H and V independently:

\[\begin{bmatrix}\delta_H\\\delta_V\end{bmatrix}={1\over2}\begin{bmatrix}c_L+c_R&c_L-c_R\\c_L-c_R&c_L+c_R\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}H\\V\end{bmatrix}.\]

The cross coefficients are equal because the mixed derivatives of the stored energy commute. This is Maxwell–Betti reciprocity. A cross displacement can be negative even though both direct compliances and stored energy are positive. The eigenvalues of this particular flexibility matrix are \(c_L,c_R\), corresponding to the two independent bar-extension directions.

Predict a sideways gauge reading and reverse the test

Take \(c_L=0.0020,\ c_R=0.0040\) mm/N. At \(V=100\) N and \(H=0\), both members carry 70.71 N. The vertical displacement is \(0.300\) mm, and the horizontal displacement is −0.100 mm. The more compliant right bar stretches farther; the joint moves left and down to accommodate both bar extensions. Half the vertical load times its displacement gives 15 N mm of strain energy. Summing the two bar energies independently gives the same result.

Apply instead \(H=100\) N and \(V=0\), assuming the slender right bar remains stable under compression. The vertical reading is −0.100 mm and horizontal motion is +0.300 mm. Thus the lateral response to the vertical test predicts the vertical response to a lateral test of equal magnitude. Equalizing bar areas would remove the cross response if the lengths and moduli matched. In practice a pin with clearance or a cable that goes slack can break the assumed linear bilateral response.

Exchange the left and right compliances. Before looking at the new graph, mark which readout should reverse sign and which should remain equal. Then set the two values alike and identify the symmetry that removes cross-coupling.

Use the course truss to test the same flexibility scaling on a larger assembly. Double every member area at fixed loads and geometry, then compare the joint displacement with the separately calculated chord force.

Run the OneSimulation truss experiment

Tutorial · identify the two member compliances from one load case

A 200 N vertical test gives +0.50 mm down and +0.10 mm right. Estimate each compliance and predict horizontal travel under simultaneous 60 N rightward and 100 N downward loading. How can one additional gauge reading test reciprocity?

Show the identification and loading prediction

Direct compliance is \(0.50/200=0.0025\) mm/N and cross compliance is \(0.10/200=0.0005\) mm/N. Adding and subtracting gives \(c_L=0.0030,\ c_R=0.0020\) mm/N. The predicted horizontal motion is

\[0.0025(60)+0.0005(100)=0.200\,\mathrm{mm}.\]

A 200 N rightward test should give +0.10 mm down. A disagreement after changing load direction suggests checking seating and the linear-elastic domain before assigning a single compliance to each bar.

Further reading · David Roylance, MIT 3.11 · truss displacements, strain energy and Castigliano’s theorem

Coupled oscillators and normal coordinates

Machine dynamics

Derive and interpret the two modes of a coupled machine, then decide which mode an applied load can excite.

From first principles

Find natural modes from the free force balance

For small motions of a conservative system with several coordinates, linear inertia forces are represented by a positive-definite mass matrix \(M\) and linear restoring forces by a symmetric stiffness matrix \(K\). Collect the free balance and try a motion in which the coordinate amplitudes stay in one fixed ratio as each oscillates at frequency \(\omega\).

\[M\ddot{\mathbf x}+K\mathbf x=0,\]
\[\mathbf x=\boldsymbol\phi\cos(\omega t)\ \Longrightarrow\ (K-\omega^2 M)\boldsymbol\phi=0 .\]

A nonzero shape \(\boldsymbol\phi\) can solve these homogeneous equations only when the coefficient matrix is singular. The determinant condition gives candidate squared frequencies, and the nullspace at each frequency gives a mode shape up to scale.

\[\det(K-\omega^2M)=0,\]
\[K\boldsymbol\phi_j=\omega_j^2M\boldsymbol\phi_j .\]

For identical masses linked by a spring, a common translation leaves that link unstretched and an opposite motion stretches it from both ends. This observation identifies common and differential shapes even before evaluating the determinant. Initial displacement and velocity select their amplitudes; the eigenproblem identifies the motions the structure can support.

Derivation and applications

Quantify the splitting of two weakly coupled modes

When two machines have the same uncoupled natural frequency, adding a weak connecting spring splits that frequency into two. Consider identical sliders of mass \(m\) each attached to ground by stiffness \(k\) and linked to each other by stiffness \(k_c\). Neglect damping and choose displacements in the same direction from equilibrium. The coupling spring responds only to the displacement difference. Adding and subtracting the two force balances therefore gives separate equations for \(s=(x_1+x_2)/2\) and \(d=(x_1-x_2)/2\).

\[m\ddot s+ks=0,\]
\[m\ddot d+(k+2k_c)d=0.\]

Release slider one at \(A\) and slider two at zero, both from rest. The two modal coordinates then both start at \(A/2\). Write \(\omega_s=\sqrt{k/m}\) and \(\omega_d=\sqrt{(k+2k_c)/m}\) and combine their solutions.

\[x_1=\tfrac A2(\cos\omega_st+\cos\omega_dt),\]
\[x_2=\tfrac A2(\cos\omega_st-\cos\omega_dt).\]

Introduce \(\bar\omega=(\omega_s+\omega_d)/2\) and \(\Delta\omega=\omega_d-\omega_s\). The sum-to-product identities separate the fast motion from slow modulation. When the split is small, the modulation resembles a repeated transfer of motion between the sliders.

\[x_1=A\cos(\bar\omega t)\cos(\Delta\omega t/2),\]
\[x_2=A\sin(\bar\omega t)\sin(\Delta\omega t/2).\]

The first minimum of the slider-one displacement envelope occurs at \(t=\pi/\Delta\omega\). Its instantaneous velocity there need not vanish because the envelope itself is changing. The envelope timing remains identifiable from repeated peaks at weak coupling.

Worked example

Time the exchange of motion

For \(m=1.00\) kg, \(k=100\) N/m and \(k_c=10.5\) N/m, the two frequencies are 10.0 and 11.0 rad/s. The first envelope minimum appears at 3.14 s and the first full recovery of its magnitude at 6.28 s. Releasing the first slider by 8.00 mm sets both modal initial amplitudes to 4.00 mm. Releasing both sliders together instead excites only the slower common motion.

Second application

Estimate coupling from a beat measurement

A second pair has mass 2.00 kg per slider and measured lower frequency 25.0 rad/s. Consecutive magnitude maxima of one slider's envelope occur 5.00 s apart. Thus

\[\Delta\omega=2\pi/5.00=1.257\,\mathrm{rad/s},\]
\[k_c=m[(25+1.257)^2-25^2]/2=64.4\,\mathrm{N/m}.\]

The independently inferred grounding stiffness is \(m\omega_s^2=1250\) N/m, making the connector about 5.2 percent as stiff.

Two masses allow two independent shapes

Two identical instruments sit on separate spring mounts and are connected by a flexible link. Pull both by the same amount and the link keeps its natural length. Pull them in opposite directions and the link stretches twice as much as either displacement. The two tests reveal distinct restoring mechanisms. A model with one equivalent spring and the sum of both masses would discard one of them.

Let \(x_1,x_2\) be horizontal displacements from equilibrium. Each mass is \(m\), each ground spring has stiffness \(k\), and the coupling spring has stiffness \(k_c\). With light springs, small displacements and no damping, the kinetic energy is \(m(\dot x_1^2+\dot x_2^2)/2\) and the elastic energy is \(k(x_1^2+x_2^2)/2+k_c(x_1-x_2)^2/2\). The coupling energy contains the difference of displacements because it is the extension between its ends.

Separate space from time

Taking the derivative of elastic energy with respect to each coordinate gives \(m\ddot x_1+(k+k_c)x_1-k_cx_2=0\) and the symmetric second equation. Look for \(\mathbf x=\boldsymbol\phi\cos\omega t\). Nonzero modal shapes solve \((\mathbf K-\omega^2\mathbf M)\boldsymbol\phi=0\). For this pair the determinant equation is

\[(k+k_c-m\omega^2)^2-k_c^2=0.\]

Taking both signs of the square root gives

\[\omega_1^2=k/m,\]
\[\omega_2^2=(k+2k_c)/m.\]

Substitution back yields shapes proportional to \((1,1)\) and \((1,-1)\). For the first, the connector transmits no incremental force. For the second, the connector acts strongly. Reversing both signs of a plotted eigenvector or multiplying it by a nonzero number describes the same physical mode.

Predict a release test at both instruments

Take \(m=5.0\) kg, \(k=2000\) N/m and \(k_c=1500\) N/m. The two angular frequencies are 20.0 and 31.62 rad/s, or 3.18 and 5.03 Hz. Pull only the first instrument 4.0 mm and release from rest. The initial displacement vector equals \(2(1,1)+2(1,-1)\) mm. Each modal amplitude starts at its corresponding value with zero initial velocity. Hence

\[x_1(t)=2\cos(20t)+2\cos(31.62t),\]
\[x_2(t)=2\cos(20t)-2\cos(31.62t)\]

in millimetres. At zero time the second displacement vanishes while the connector exerts 6.0 N on its mass. Its initial acceleration is 1.2 m/s² toward the first instrument’s displacement. This apparent transfer of motion needs neither forcing nor damping: modal displacements add and cancel differently at the two observation points.

Distinguish available modes from visible modes

Define average and relative coordinates \(q_s=(x_1+x_2)/2\) and \(q_a=(x_1-x_2)/2\). Adding or subtracting the original equations decouples them. Equal external forces excite only \(q_s\); equal and opposite forces excite only \(q_a\). Taking the signal \((x_1+x_2)/2\) likewise hides the antisymmetric mode. A missing spectral peak can therefore reveal the symmetry of a load or sensor arrangement, even though the corresponding eigenmode still exists.

If the two masses differ, the simple sum and difference cease to diagonalise the equations. The general route is to form the physical mass and stiffness matrices and solve the symmetric generalised eigenproblem. Distinct modes of an undamped conservative system are orthogonal with respect to mass, \(\boldsymbol\phi_i^T\mathbf M\boldsymbol\phi_j=0\). This weighted orthogonality is what permits a measured motion or distributed load to be projected into separate modal coordinates.

Predict which mode responds to adding rotary inertia

For a unit mode shape \(\phi\), the quotient \(\omega^2=\phi^TK\phi/(\phi^TM\phi)\) compares stored elastic energy with inertia. Increasing inertia at a coordinate matters most when that coordinate has appreciable motion in the mode. A frame with three tip coordinates offers a way to check this reasoning beyond the symmetric two-mass illustration.

Compare two changes to the same frame: double only the tip mass from 200 to 400 kg, then restore it and double only the tip rotary inertia from 60 to 120 kg m². Predict whether either change can raise a natural frequency for the same stiffness and compute the fractional shift of the third mode in each case. Use the negligible-member-mass row throughout. Check the frame frequency measurements.

Derive bounce and pitch modes of a supported machine

A long machine supported on two mounts can translate and rotate as a nearly rigid body even if there is negligible frame bending. Let the mass centre move vertically by \(z\) and rotate by small angle \(\theta\), with positive rotation raising a point to the right. Vertical spring deflections relative to ground are \(z-a\theta\) on the left and \(z+b\theta\) on the right. Measure displacements from static equilibrium. The kinetic energy is \((m\dot z^2+I_G\dot\theta^2)/2\), and the spring energy is \([k_L(z-a\theta)^2+k_R(z+b\theta)^2]/2\).

Differentiating potential energy gives stiffness terms \(K_{zz}=k_L+k_R,\ K_{\theta\theta}=k_La^2+k_Rb^2,\ K_{z\theta}=-k_La+k_Rb\). The mass matrix is diagonal because translation was measured at the centre of mass. A vertical translation produces a moment whenever the weighted mount locations fail to balance, and a pure rotation then also produces a vertical force by reciprocity. If \(k_La=k_Rb\) the modes are pure translation and pure pitch. Equal spring rates alone do not guarantee decoupling when the lever arms differ.

For free motion solve \((K-\omega^2 M)\phi=0\). In mixed coordinates, metres and radians label different displacement entries, so a numerical component ratio needs units or a stated length scale. Measured vertical motions at two actual sensor locations provide an unambiguous observable mode shape.

Separate a heaving mode from a rocking mode

Let \(m=200\) kg, \(I_G=40\,\mathrm{kg\,m^2}\), \(a=b=0.50\) m and mount rates \(k_L=30000,\ k_R=50000\,\mathrm{N/m}\). The stiffness entries are 80000 N/m, 20000 N m/rad and 10000 N in the mixed term. Setting the determinant to zero gives \((80000-200\lambda)(20000-40\lambda)-10000^2=0\) with \(\lambda=\omega^2\). Dividing through by 8000 yields

\[\lambda^2-900\lambda+187500=0.\]

The squared frequencies are 327.5 and 572.5 s⁻², giving 2.88 and 3.81 Hz.

For the lower mode the first row implies

\[\theta/z=-(80000-200\times327.5)/10000=-1.45\,\mathrm{rad/m}.\]

If mass-centre motion has 1 mm amplitude, the mount-point displacements are 1.725 and 0.275 mm, in phase but strongly unequal. The higher mode gives \(\theta/z=+3.45\,\mathrm{rad/m}\), so the left point now moves oppositely to the right. An accelerometer placed close to a mode’s nodal point can scarcely detect it although that mode remains physically present. For the low mode the vertical node lies about \(1/1.45=0.69\) m to the right of the centre, beyond the right mount. Two well-separated measurement points make the rotational contribution identifiable.

A static load experiment can identify the stiffness coupling before a modal test. Apply a known vertical force \(F\) at the centre and no external moment. The pitch-equilibrium equation gives \(\theta=-K_{z\theta}z/K_{\theta\theta}=-0.50z\) rad per metre for these mounts. Substitution into the vertical balance leaves effective static stiffness \(80000-10000^2/20000=75000\,\mathrm{N/m}\). Under 150 N centre load, predicted motion is 2.00 mm and pitch −0.0010 rad, giving left and right mount readings of 2.50 and 1.50 mm. A centre gauge alone would only indicate an apparent softening, while the unequal pair exposes the rotational coupling that later appears in the two mode shapes.

Find a sensor position that can see a mode

Use the first three bending modes of an ideal simply supported uniform beam as a continuous analogue of coupled oscillators. A local displacement transducer reports a modal coordinate multiplied by the signed shape at the measurement location.
